At the museum, someone might be explaining the next art walk, and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time following what someone is saying if they wrote it down for you to read?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. If it’s written down, I can look back at it. When someone at the museum is just telling me about the next art walk, even if I’m looking right at them, I might miss the middle of it and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to follow and understand a conversation better if someone spoke more slowly to you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s really about speed. It’s more like they’ll be talking right to me, like at the museum about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle of it. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying when they use complex sentences or complicated vocabulary?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it being about big words or complicated sentences. It’s more just… sometimes I don’t catch what was said right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying when they talk to you in a quiet room with no distractions?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can still happen even if it’s quiet. That’s kind of what brings me in—someone at the museum will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle of what they said. So I end up asking them to run it by me again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them, but then I realize I missed a chunk of what they said. I end up having to ask them to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you weren't paying attention when you missed what they said, or did you just not understand it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m not staying with it, even though I’m looking right at them. I usually understand them fine, but somewhere in the middle I just miss part of what they said, and then I have to ask them to go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this happens with all kinds of conversations, or just in certain situations or environments?\n\nPatient: It’s most noticeable in situations like at the museum, when someone’s talking right to me about the next art walk or something like that. I’ll be looking at them and trying to listen, but then I realize I missed the middle part and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following conversations when there's background noise?\n\nPatient: I notice it mostly at the museum when someone’s talking right to me about something like the next art walk. I’m looking at them, and I think I’m listening, but then I realize I missed the middle of what they said and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're actually hearing the words but not processing them, or do you think you're just not hearing the words at all when you miss part of what someone says?\n\nPatient: I think I’m hearing them at first, but I’m not really taking it all in. Like at the museum, someone will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following conversations when there's no background noise?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can happen even without much noise. Like I said, someone at the museum can be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but I’ll still miss the middle of what they said and need them to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying, or is it more that you forget what they said after they've finished talking?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I don’t really take in the middle of what they’re saying while they’re talking. I’m looking at them, and I hear them start, but then afterward I realize I missed a chunk of it. So I have to ask them to say it again, like when people at the museum are telling me about the next art walk.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying immediately after they say it, or is it more that you can't recall what they said a little while later?\n\nPatient: It’s more immediate, I think. At the museum, someone might be explaining the next art walk, and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time following what someone is saying if they wrote it down for you to read?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. If it’s written down, I can look back at it. It’s more like they’ll be talking right to me, like at the museum about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle of it. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying when they use complex sentences or complicated vocabulary?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it being about big words or complicated sentences. I usually understand them fine, but somewhere in the middle I just miss part of what they said, and then I have to ask them to go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this happens with all kinds of conversations, or just in certain situations or environments?\n\nPatient: It’s most noticeable in situations like at the museum, when someone’s talking right to me about the next art walk or something like that. I’ll be looking at them and trying to listen, but then I realize I missed the middle part and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following conversations when there's background noise?\n\nPatient: I notice it mostly at the museum when someone’s talking right to me about something like the next art walk. I’m looking at them, and I think I’m listening, but then I realize I missed the middle of what they said and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're actually hearing the words but not processing them, or do you think you're just not hearing the words at all when you miss part of what someone says?\n\nPatient: I think I’m hearing them at first, but I’m not really taking it all in. Like at the museum, someone will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following conversations when there's no background noise?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can happen even without much noise. Like I said, someone at the museum can be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but I’ll still miss the middle of what they said and need them to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying, or is it more that you forget what they said after they've finished talking?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I don’t really take in the middle of what they’re saying while they’re talking. I’m looking at them, and I hear them start, but then afterward I realize I missed a chunk of it. So I have to ask them to say it again, like when people at the museum are telling me about the next art walk.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying immediately after they say it, or is it more that you can't recall what they said a little while later?\n\nPatient: It’s more immediate, I think. Like, they’ll finish saying it and I realize I didn’t catch the middle part, even though I was looking at them. At the museum, someone might be explaining the next art walk, and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time following what someone is saying if they wrote it down for you to read?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. If it’s written down, I can look back at it. When someone at the museum is just telling me about the next art walk, even if I’m looking right at them, I might miss the middle of it and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to follow and understand a conversation better if someone spoke more slowly to you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s really about speed. It’s more like they’ll be talking right to me, like at the museum about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle of it. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying when they use complex sentences or complicated vocabulary?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it being about big words or complicated sentences. It’s more just… sometimes I don’t catch what was said right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying when they talk to you in a quiet room with no distractions?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can still happen even if it’s quiet. That’s kind of what brings me in—someone at the museum will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle of what they said. So I end up asking them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to repeat back what someone just said to you, right after they finish talking?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I can repeat part of it, but not all of it. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them, but then I realize I missed the middle. So if they asked me to repeat it back, I’d probably have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding speech when it's presented in a way that doesn't allow you to see the speaker's lips or face?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed if it’s specifically worse when I can’t see someone’s face or lips. I’d have to pay attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have a harder time understanding what someone is saying when you're not looking at them, like when you're talking on the phone?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I use the phone some, but I haven’t really noticed whether it’s harder because I’m not looking at the person. I’d have to pay closer attention to that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I end up having to ask them to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you weren't paying attention when you missed what they said, or did you just not understand it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m not staying with it, even though I’m looking right at them. I’ll be looking at them and trying to listen, but then I realize I missed the middle part and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following conversations when there's background noise?\n\nPatient: I notice it mostly at the museum when someone’s talking right to me about something like the next art walk. I’m looking at them, and I think I’m listening, but then I realize I missed the middle of what they said and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're actually hearing the words but not processing them, or do you think you're just not hearing the words at all when you miss part of what someone says?\n\nPatient: I think I’m hearing them at first, but I’m not really taking it all in. Like at the museum, someone will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following conversations when there's no background noise?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can happen even without much noise. Like I said, someone at the museum can be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but I’ll still miss the middle of what they said and need them to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying, or is it more that you forget what they said after they've finished talking?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I don’t really take in the middle of what they’re saying while they’re talking. I’m looking at them, and I hear them start, but then afterward I realize I missed a chunk of it. So I have to ask them to say it again, like when people at the museum are telling me about the next art walk.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying immediately after they say it, or is it more that you can't recall what they said a little while later?\n\nPatient: It’s more immediate, I think. Like, they’ll finish saying it and I realize I didn’t catch the middle part, even though I was looking at them. At the museum, someone might be explaining the next art walk, and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time following what someone is saying if they wrote it down for you to read?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. If it’s written down, I can look back at it. When someone at the museum is just telling me about the next art walk, even if I’m looking right at them, I might miss the middle of it and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to follow and understand a conversation better if someone spoke more slowly to you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s really about speed. It’s more like they’ll be talking right to me, like at the museum about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle of it. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying when they use complex sentences or complicated vocabulary?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it being about big words or complicated sentences. It’s more just… sometimes I don’t catch what was said right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying when they talk to you in a quiet room with no distractions?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can still happen even if it’s quiet. So I end up asking them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to repeat back what someone just said to you, right after they finish talking?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I can repeat part of it, but not all of it. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them, but then I realize I missed the middle. So if they asked me to repeat it back, I’d probably have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding speech when it's presented in a way that doesn't allow you to see the speaker's lips or face?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed if it’s specifically worse when I can’t see someone’s face or lips. I’d have to pay attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have a harder time understanding what someone is saying when you're not looking at them, like when you're talking on the phone?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I use the phone some, but I haven’t really noticed whether it’s harder because I’m not looking at the person. I’d have to pay closer attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time understanding what someone is saying if you couldn't see their face or lips while they are talking?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed that not seeing someone’s face or lips makes it easier for me. I’d say I’m just not sure either way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I’ve been having trouble when people are talking to me. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them, but then I realize I missed a chunk of what they said. I end up having to ask them to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you weren't paying attention when you missed what they said, or did you just not understand it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more that I’m not staying with it, even though I’m looking right at them. I usually understand them fine, but somewhere in the middle I just miss part of what they said, and then I have to ask them to go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this happens with all kinds of conversations, or just in certain situations or environments?\n\nPatient: It’s most noticeable in situations like at the museum, when someone’s talking right to me about the next art walk or something like that. I’ll be looking at them and trying to listen, but then I realize I missed the middle part and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following conversations when there's background noise?\n\nPatient: I notice it mostly at the museum when someone’s talking right to me about something like the next art walk. I’m looking at them, and I think I’m listening, but then I realize I missed the middle of what they said and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're actually hearing the words but not processing them, or do you think you're just not hearing the words at all when you miss part of what someone says?\n\nPatient: I think I’m hearing them at first, but I’m not really taking it all in. Like at the museum, someone will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following conversations when there's no background noise?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can happen even without much noise. Like I said, someone at the museum can be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but I’ll still miss the middle of what they said and need them to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying, or is it more that you forget what they said after they've finished talking?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I don’t really take in the middle of what they’re saying while they’re talking. I’m looking at them, and I hear them start, but then afterward I realize I missed a chunk of it. So I have to ask them to say it again, like when people at the museum are telling me about the next art walk.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying immediately after they say it, or is it more that you can't recall what they said a little while later?\n\nPatient: It’s more immediate, I think. Like, they’ll finish saying it and I realize I didn’t catch the middle part, even though I was looking at them. At the museum, someone might be explaining the next art walk, and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time following what someone is saying if they wrote it down for you to read?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. If it’s written down, I can look back at it. When someone at the museum is just telling me about the next art walk, even if I’m looking right at them, I might miss the middle of it and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to follow and understand a conversation better if someone spoke more slowly to you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s really about speed. It’s more like they’ll be talking right to me, like at the museum about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle of it. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying when they use complex sentences or complicated vocabulary?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it being about big words or complicated sentences. It’s more just… sometimes I don’t catch what was said right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying when they talk to you in a quiet room with no distractions?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can still happen even if it’s quiet. That’s kind of what brings me in—someone at the museum will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle of what they said. So I end up asking them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to repeat back what someone just said to you, right after they finish talking?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I can repeat part of it, but not all of it. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them, but then I realize I missed the middle. I haven’t noticed if it’s specifically worse when I can’t see someone’s face or lips. I’d have to pay attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have a harder time understanding what someone is saying when you're not looking at them, like when you're talking on the phone?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I use the phone some, but I haven’t really noticed whether it’s harder because I’m not looking at the person. I usually understand them fine, but somewhere in the middle I just miss part of what they said, and then I have to ask them to go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that this happens with all kinds of conversations, or just in certain situations or environments?\n\nPatient: It’s most noticeable in situations like at the museum, when someone’s talking right to me about the next art walk or something like that. I’ll be looking at them and trying to listen, but then I realize I missed the middle part and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following conversations when there's background noise?\n\nPatient: I notice it mostly at the museum when someone’s talking right to me about something like the next art walk. I’m looking at them, and I think I’m listening, but then I realize I missed the middle of what they said and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're actually hearing the words but not processing them, or do you think you're just not hearing the words at all when you miss part of what someone says?\n\nPatient: I think I’m hearing them at first, but I’m not really taking it all in. Like at the museum, someone will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’ll be looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle part and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble following conversations when there's no background noise?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can happen even without much noise. Like I said, someone at the museum can be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but I’ll still miss the middle of what they said and need them to repeat it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying, or is it more that you forget what they said after they've finished talking?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I don’t really take in the middle of what they’re saying while they’re talking. I’m looking at them, and I hear them start, but then afterward I realize I missed a chunk of it. So I have to ask them to say it again, like when people at the museum are telling me about the next art walk.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying immediately after they say it, or is it more that you can't recall what they said a little while later?\n\nPatient: It’s more immediate, I think. Like, they’ll finish saying it and I realize I didn’t catch the middle part, even though I was looking at them. At the museum, someone might be explaining the next art walk, and I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time following what someone is saying if they wrote it down for you to read?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes. If it’s written down, I can look back at it. When someone at the museum is just telling me about the next art walk, even if I’m looking right at them, I might miss the middle of it and have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be able to follow and understand a conversation better if someone spoke more slowly to you?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s really about speed. It’s more like they’ll be talking right to me, like at the museum about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle of it. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying when they use complex sentences or complicated vocabulary?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it being about big words or complicated sentences. It’s more just… sometimes I don’t catch what was said right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying when they talk to you in a quiet room with no distractions?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can still happen even if it’s quiet. That’s kind of what brings me in—someone at the museum will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them, but then I realize I missed the middle of what they said. Like at the museum, someone will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m looking right at them, but then I realize I missed the middle. So if they asked me to repeat it back, I’d probably have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding speech when it's presented in a way that doesn't allow you to see the speaker's lips or face?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed if it’s specifically worse when I can’t see someone’s face or lips. I’d have to pay attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have a harder time understanding what someone is saying when you're not looking at them, like when you're talking on the phone?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I use the phone some, but I haven’t really noticed whether it’s harder because I’m not looking at the person. I’d have to pay closer attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier time understanding what someone is saying if you couldn't see their face or lips while they are talking?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed that not seeing someone’s face or lips makes it easier for me. I’d say I’m just not sure either way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have an easier or harder time understanding what someone is saying if you were talking to them in a completely dark room?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’m looking at them, and I hear them start, but then afterward I realize I missed a chunk of it. So I have to ask them to say it again, like when people at the museum are telling me about the next art walk.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying immediately after they say it, or is it more that you can't recall what they said a little while later?\n\nPatient: It’s more immediate, I think. If it’s written down, I can look back at it. Then I have to ask them to run it by me again.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying when they use complex sentences or complicated vocabulary?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it being about big words or complicated sentences. It’s more just… sometimes I don’t catch what was said right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding what people are saying when they talk to you in a quiet room with no distractions?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can still happen even if it’s quiet. I’d have to pay attention to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have a harder time understanding what someone is saying when you're not looking at them, like when you're talking on the phone?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure.
